np.ndarray:\n    outcomes = np.zeros(len(trials), dtype=np.int8)\n    reward_ptr = 0\n    for trial_idx, (start, stop) in enumerate(trials):\n        start_t = trial_times[start]\n        stop_t =        position_cm < zone_start,\n        position_cm - zone_start,\n        np.where(position_cm > zone_end, position_cm - zone_end, 0.0),\n    )\n    bins = np.full(distance.shape, 6, bins[(distance > 0.0) & (distance <= 10.0)] = 4\n    bins[(distance > 10.0) & (distance <= 50.0)] = 5\n    return bins\n\n\ndef discretize_absolute_position(position_cm: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:\n right=False).astype(np.int16)\n\n\ndef discretize_speed(speed_cm_s: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:\n    bins = np.full(speed_cm_s.shape, 4, dtype=np.int16)\n    bins[speed_cm_s < 2.0] = 0\n    bins[(speed_cm_s >= 2.0) & (speed_cm_s < 10.0)] = 1\n    bins[(speed_cm_s >= 10.0) & (speed_cm_s < 20.0)] = 2\n    bins[(speed_cm_s >= 20.0) & (speed_cm_s <= 40.0)] = 3\n    return bins\n\n\ndef Raw imaging + VR -> `sess` via `create_sess` / `append_session_data`.\n    45→  2. `sess.align_VR_to_2P()` aligns Unity VR to imaging frame times.\n    46→  3. Behavior timeseries added directly from aligned VR (`licks`, `rewards`, `speed`).\n    47→  4. dF/F is not stored in the raw `sess`; it is computed later by `multi_anim_sess` using `preprocessing.dff`.\n    48→  5. Deconvolved calcium `events` are derived from dF/F and used for the decoder notebook.\n    49→- The code is for 2-photon calcium imaging, not electrophysiology.\n    50→- Delta F over F does need to be computed in the original analysis pipeline.\n    51→- Cell curation is not an ephys spike-quality filter. Instead, ROIs are manually curated in the Suite2p GUI, which updates `iscell.npy`; `sess.load_suite2p_data()` loads that curation.\n    52→- `multi_anim_sess_README.md` states each sample is one imaging frame at about 15.5 Hz (~64.5 ms/sample), and `trial_matrices` are spatial bins of 10 cm across 0-450 cm.\n    53→- Decoder-relevant continuous variables in reference code:\n    54→  - neural: `sess.timeseries['events']`\n    55→  - position: `sess.vr_data['pos']`\n    56→  - reward-relative position: computed from reward-zone start position per trial\n    57→  - trial id: repeated sample-wise from trial index\n    58→  - speed: `sess.timeseries['speed']`\n    59→  - rewards: `sess.timeseries['rewards']`\n    60→  - licks: `sess.timeseries['licks']`, with values >1 clipped to 1 and invalid sensors masked\n    61→- Important masking/alignment details from `get_timeseries_data`:\n    62→  - Only samples from `trial_start_inds` to `teleport_inds` are kept.\n    63→  - Trial slices use `start-1:stop-1`, so reference indexing is effectively 1-based for stored trial boundary indices.\n    64→  - Samples with NaNs in `events`, low speed (`<2 cm/s` by default), or invalid lick samples are removed for the paper’s GLM/decoder analyses.\n    65→  - Reward and omission signals are represented as state variables that switch after reward delivery or reward-zone entry, respectively, within a trial.\n    66→- The paper’s Figure 3 decoder notebook decodes circular reward-relative position from deconvolved events, and down-samples observations to match position occupancy across trial sets before training.\n    67→\n    68→---\n    69→\n    70→## Step 2: Dataset Exploration\n    71→**Status**: COMPLETE\n    72→\n    73→### Data Structure\n    74→- Top level 77→- Each subject folder contains one NWB file per session, named like `sub-m3_ses-01_behavior+ophys.nwb`.\n    78→- Total files in `data/`: 152 NWB session files.\n    79→- Native format: NWB/HDF5, `scanning`, `speed`, `teleport`, `trial number`, `trial_start`\n    85→  - Ophys interfaces:\n    86→    - `Fluorescence/plane0/data`: frames x ROIs raw fluorescence\n    87→    - `Neuropil/plane0/data`: frames x ROIs neuropil fluorescence\n    88→    - `Deconvolved/plane0/data`: frames x ROIs deconvolved activity\n    89→    - `ImageSegmentation/PlaneSegmentation`: ROI table with `pixel_mask`, `iscell`, `planeIdx`\n    90→- Behavioral streams use explicit timestamps; ophys series use `starting_time` with frame rate attribute.\n    91→- Imaging plane metadata indicates brain region `hippocampus, CA1`.\n    92→- Important native encodings observed:\n    93→  - `environment`: values include `0`, `1` and invalid pre-sync `-1`\n    94→  - `trial number`: includes invalid pre-sync `-1`\n    95→  - `position`: includes invalid pre-sync `-500`\n    96→  - `reward_zone`: integer-coded values `0..8` observed in sample files\n    97→  - `iscell`: shape `(n_rois, 2)` in sample files, consistent with Suite2p export `[is_cell_flag, probability]`\n    98→- Example session (`sub-m3_ses-01`):\n    99→  - 33,913 frames\n   100→  - 2,929 total ROIs before `iscell` filtering\n   101→  - frame rate 15.5078125 Hz\n   102→  - behavior timestamps span 0 to 2186.77 s\n   103→\n   104→### Dataset Size (from data files)\n   105→| Statistic | Value |\n   113→\n   114→---\n   115→\n   116→## Step 3: Reference Text Reading\n   117→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   118→\n   119→### Expected Statistics (from paper/methods)\n   120→| Statistic | Value | Source Quote |\n   121→|-----------|-------|--------------|\n   122→| Neurons (total) | Not stated directly for switch cohort; NWB should reflect curated switch-task cells | Paper gives per-session range rather than dataset total |\n   123→| Neurons / session | 155-2172 putative pyramidal neurons/session | “This approach yielded 155–2172 putative pyramidal neurons per session” |\n   124→| Subjects | 11 switch-task mice in main cohort | “Each mouse encountered a different starting reward zone… (n = 11 mice)” |\n   125→| Sessions / subject | 14 planned imaging days; m11 starts on day 3 | “task… for a total of 14 days”; “except m11, for whom imaging started on day 3” |\n   126→| Trials (total) | 12,376 imaged trials across 11 switch mice (from lick-QC context) | “81 out of 12,376 trials removed across 11 switch mice” |\n   127→| Trials / session | 80-100 targeted; mean 80.5 +/- 7.4 across all imaging days in 14 mice | “We targeted 80–100 trials per session… (mean ± s.d., 80.5 ± 7.4 trials…)” |\n   128→| Neural data time bin | ~0.0645 s per frame (~15.5 Hz) | “sampled at ~15.5 Hz”; “0.0645 s imaging frame samples” |\n   129→| Behavior data time bin | Same as neural, sampled at imaging frame rate | “All behavioral and neural time series were sampled at ~15.5 Hz” |\n   130→| Reward rate | ~85% rewarded, ~15% omitted | “Reward was randomly omitted on approximately 15% of trials” |\n   131→| Track length | 450 cm | “450 cm linear track” |\n   132→| Reward zone size / locations | 50 cm; A=80-130, B=200-250, C=320-370 cm | “zone A, 80–130 cm; zone B, 200–250 cm; zone C, 320–370 cm” |\n   133→| Switch timing | After 30 trials on switch days | “Each switch occurred after 30 trials” |\n   134→| Spatial binning | 45 bins of 10 cm | “binned the 450 cm linear track into 45 bins of 10 cm each” |\n   135→| Interneuron exclusion | 0.42 +/- 0.85% of cells excluded | “excluding 0.42 ± 0.85% of cells” |\n   136→| Lick-error trial exclusion | ~0.65% of imaged trials; 81/12,376 | “~0.65% of all imaged trials, n = 81 out of 12,376” |\n   137→| RR-cell fraction | 16.3 +/- 5.3% of place cells over switch days | “16.3 ± 5.3% of place cells averaged over all switch days” |\n   138→| Decoder result | RR cells decode RR position above shuffle after switch; z-scored decode >2 from about -104.5 to +152.7 cm | “only the RR population decoded… better than shuffle… range of z-scored decode >2: -104.5 ± 20.1 cm to +152.7 ± 22.9 cm” |\n   139→\n   140→\n   141→### Processing Details\n   142→- Task structure:\n   143→  - 450 cm linear VR track with hidden 50 cm reward zone.\n   144→  - Reward zones A/B/C are fixed track spans: A `80-130`, B `200-250`, C `320-370` cm.\n   145→  - On switch days, reward zone changes after 30 trials.\n   146→  - ENV2 introduced on day 8; two mice (`m17`, `m18`) started in ENV2.\n   147→- Temporal alignment and sampling:\n   148→  - Neural and behavioral data are analyzed at the imaging frame rate (~15.5 Hz, ~64.5 ms).\n   149→  - Reference code aligns Unity VR data to imaging frames before any later analyses.\n   150→  - For many analyses, only in-trial samples are used; teleport period treatment depends on session/imaging settings.\n   151→- Neural processing:\n   152→  - Fluorescence is motion-corrected and segmented with Suite2P.\n   153→  - dF/F is computed independently within each trial using a maximin baseline with a 20 s window.\n   154→  - dF/F is smoothed with a 2-sample Gaussian.\n   155→  - Deconvolved `events` are extracted with OASIS and are used for decoder and place-cell analyses.\n   156→- Behavioral processing:\n   157→  - Activity at running speeds `<2 cm/s` is excluded from spatial neural analyses and RR decoding.\n   158→  - Licks are binary after correction; corrupted lick trials are set to NaN and excluded from licking analyses.\n   159→  - Reward outcome is trial-specific with random omissions on ~15% of trials.\n   160→- Decoder-specific details:\n   161→  - Target in the paper is circular reward-relative position (`-pi` to `pi`), aligned to reward-zone start.\n   162→  - Tenfold CV, 90% train / 10% test.\n   163→  - Occupancy downsampling matches each RR position bin (`2*pi/45`, about 10 cm).\n   164→  - Shuffles independently circularly shift each neuron’s session timeseries.\n   165→\n   166→### Curation Steps\n   167→\n   168→**Neuron curation rules**:\n   169→- Manual Suite2P curation removes ROIs that:\n   170→  - contain multiple somata or dendrites\n   171→  - lack visually obvious transients\n   172→  - appear overexpressed\n   173→  - show high continuous fluorescence fluctuation typical of putative interneurons\n   174→- Additional putative interneurons are excluded if corr(dF/F, running speed) > 0.5.\n   175→- Multi-plane mice pool planes for most analyses.\n   176→\n   177→**Trial curation rules**:\n   178→- Random reward omissions are part of the task and are not discarded by default.\n   179→- Lick-analysis trials are removed when >30% of imaging-frame samples in the trial have cumulative lick count >2.\n   180→- Many analyses exclude samples with running speed <2 cm/s rather than dropping entire trials.\n   181→- Some analyses use:\n   182→  - pre-switch 30 trials\n   183→  - final 30 post-switch trials\n   184→  - first vs second half of trials on non-switch days\n   185→\n   186→### Decoders Trained\n   187→| Decoded variable | Accuracy |\n   188→| Reward-relative position (paper decoder) | No single scalar value stated in text; qualitatively above shuffle for RR cells after switch, with z-scored decode >2 spanning about -104.5 to +152.7 cm around reward |\n   189→\n   190→---\n   191→\n   192→## Step 4: Check for Consistency\n   193→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   194→\n   195→### Discrepancies Found\n   196→| Topic | Code says | Data shows | Paper says | Resolution |\n   197→|-------|-----------|------------|------------|------------|\n   198→| Session container | Code operates on a `sess` object produced earlier, then adds `dff` and `events` | NWB files already store aligned behavior plus `Fluorescence`, `Neuropil`, and `Deconvolved` | Paper describes the same pipeline: align VR to imaging, compute dF/F, deconvolve | Treat NWB as an exported `sess`-equivalent. Use NWB aligned behavior streams and map `Deconvolved/plane0/data` to reference `events` unless a later check shows mismatch. |\n   199→| Reward-zone labeling | Code infers reward-zone coordinates from scene names like `Env2_LocationA_to_B` | NWB `reward_zone` samples are not A/B/C labels; values increase within-zone and are only meaningful as `>0` occupancy | Paper defines zone A/B/C by track coordinates and describes scene-based switches | Parse scene/location from NWB `identifier`; use `reward_zone > 0` only as occupancy / entry signal, and derive A/B/C labels from scene metadata for per-trial zone identity. |\n   200→| Trial timing | Code uses `trial_start_inds` / `teleport_inds` and slices `start-1:stop-1` | NWB stores frame-wise `trial_start`, `teleport`, `trial number`, timestamps | Paper defines trials as laps ending in teleport period | Reconstruct trial start/end frame indices from NWB behavior streams so trial segmentation matches the reference semantics. |\n   201→| Environment identity | Code uses `morph` from VR data, with `Env1 -> 0`, `Env2 -> 1` | NWB `environment` contains valid values `0/1` plus pre-sync invalid `-1` | Paper describes two environments ENV1 / ENV2 | Use valid `environment` values directly from NWB after excluding invalid pre-sync samples, and cross-check against scene identifier. |\n   202→| ROI curation count | Code/paper report 155-2172 putative pyramidal neurons/session after curation | NWB `iscell[:,0]==1` gives 155-2341 curated ROIs/session; max session is multi-plane `m18 ses03` | Paper states 155-2172/session and excludes additional putative interneurons by speed correlation | Likely requires additional reference-style interneuron exclusion and possibly careful handling of multi-plane pooled ROIs. This becomes a planned consistency check for conversion. |\n   203→| dF/F availability | Code computes dF/F from `F`/`Fneu` with per-trial maximin baseline | NWB exports `Fluorescence`, `Neuropil`, and `Deconvolved`, but not stored `dff` | Paper explicitly defines dF/F computation and deconvolution | For decoder inputs we mainly need neural activity. Candidate paths are: (1) use NWB `Deconvolved` if it matches reference `events`; (2) recompute dF/F / deconvolution from `Fluorescence` and `Neuropil` if necessary. Need later sanity check against raw NWB signals and paper stats. |\n   204→| Total session count | Task schedule implies 14 days per mouse | NWB has 152 sessions across 11 mice | Paper notes m11 started imaging on day 3 | This matches: 11*14 = 154 planned switch sessions, minus 2 missing early m11 sessions = 152. |\n   205→\n   206→---\n   207→\n   208→## Step 5: Mapping Planning\n   209→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   210→\n   211→### Variable Mapping\n   212→| Source Variable | Target Field | Transform | Reference Code Function(s) | Notes |\n   213→|-----------------|--------------|-----------|----------------------------|-------|\n   214→| `processing/ophys/Deconvolved/plane0/data` | `neural` | Select curated ROIs (`iscell[:,0] == 1`), transpose to `(neurons, time)` per trial, slice `trial_start:teleport` | `glmUtils.get_timeseries_data`, `utilities.multi_anim_sess` | Candidate equivalent of reference `sess.timeseries['events']`; later sanity-check against raw NWB. |\n   215→| `processing/behavior/BehavioralTimeSeries/position` timestamps | `input[0]` = `time_from_trial_start_s` | `timestamps - timestamps[trial_start]` within each trial | Reference alignment is imaging-frame synchronized behavior | Continuous, time-varying, aligned to trial start as required by decoder task. |\n   216→| `processing/behavior/BehavioralTimeSeries/environment` | `input[1]` = `environment` | Per-trial constant repeated across frames; valid values only (`0/1`) | `behavior.get_trial_types` (`morph`) | Cross-check against scene identifier; encode `ENV1=0`, `ENV2=1`. |\n   217→| Reconstructed within-session trial index | `input[2]` = `trial_number` | 0-based per-trial index repeated across frames | `glmUtils.get_timeseries_data` (`trials`) | Use reconstructed trial order from `trial_start` events, not raw `trial number` during teleport. |\n   218→| Previous trial reward outcome | `input[3]` = `previous_trial_outcome` | For trial `t`, use reward outcome of trial `t-1`; first trial defaults to `0`; repeat across frames | Consistent with task spec; related to `behavior.get_trial_types` | Binary, `0=omitted/unrewarded`, `1=rewarded`. |\n   219→| `position` + trial reward-zone coordinates | `output[0]` = `distance_to_reward_zone_bin` | Signed distance to nearest point in active reward zone: negative before zone, zero inside, positive after zone; discretize into 7 bins from task spec | Paper reward-zone definitions + scene parsing | Use per-trial active zone A/B/C inferred from scene / switch status. |\n   220→| `position` | `output[1]` = `absolute_position_bin` | Bin position on `0-450 cm` track into 5 equal bins | Paper track length 450 cm | Use only in-trial frames; exclude teleport. |\n   221→| `speed` | `output[2]` = `speed_bin` | Discretize cm/s into `<2`, `2-10`, `10-20`, `20-40`, `>40` | `glmUtils.get_timeseries_data` speed stream; paper threshold at 2 cm/s | Do not drop low-speed frames because they are a decoder target class here. |\n   222→| `lick` | `output[3]` = `lick` | Clip cumulative lick counts to binary `0/1`; bad-lick trials dropped by QC rule | `glmUtils.get_timeseries_data`, paper lick QC | Target is time-varying binary lick. |\n   223→| Scene-derived active reward zone | `output[4]` = `reward_zone_location` | Map `A/B/C -> 0/1/2`; repeat across frames in the trial | `behavior.get_reward_zones` | Derived from NWB `identifier` scene string and switch-after-30 rule. |\n   224→| Trial reward delivery from `BehavioralTimeSeries/Reward` | `output[5]` = `reward_outcome` | `1` if any reward event timestamp falls within trial, else `0`; repeat across frames | `behavior.get_trial_types`, `rewardAnalysis.get_reward_inds` | Uses actual delivered reward, matching decoder task. |\n   225→\n   226→### Key Decisions\n   227→1. **Neural signal = deconvolved activity**: The reference decoder and place-cell pipeline use deconvolved calcium activity (`events`). NWB already exports `Deconvolved`, so this is the closest native match.\n   228→2. **Trial-aligned frames exclude teleport period**: Reference code keeps samples from trial start until teleport onset. I will reconstruct trials from `trial_start` impulses to the next `teleport` impulse, excluding teleport frames.\n   229→3. **Use native imaging-frame resolution**: The reference data are sampled at ~15.5 Hz and behavior is already synchronized to this grid. No rebinning in time unless a later validation forces it.\n   230→4. **Per-trial constants will be repeated across time**: Although some decoder variables are per-trial, repeating them across each trial’s time axis gives a uniform `(n_variables, n_timepoints)` representation and matches the decoder’s time-varying format.\n   231→5. **Scene identifier drives reward-zone identity**: NWB `reward_zone` is not the A/B/C label. Active reward-zone identity will be parsed from the session identifier (for example `Env1_LocationA_to_B`, `Env1_B_to_Env2_C`) and the 30-trial switch rule from the paper/code.\n   232→6. **Bad lick trials will be removed**: Because lick is a required decoder output and NaNs are undesirable, trials meeting the paper’s lick-sensor failure rule will be excluded rather than carried with missing labels.\n   233→7. **Initial neuron filter = `iscell[:,0] == 1`**: This matches Suite2p manual curation. Additional interneuron-style exclusions remain a consistency check because of the modest count mismatch with the paper.\n   234→8. **No speed-based sample exclusion in final conversion**: The paper excludes `<2 cm/s` for spatial analyses, but here speed itself is an output class. I will preserve these frames and encode low speed as class `0`.\n   235→9. **Absolute position uses track-only interval**: Position bins will cover the 450 cm corridor only; teleport frames are excluded, so no teleport-specific bins are needed.\n   236→\n   237→### Planned Sanity Checks\n   238→- [ ] Check 1: For a chosen session/trial, verify `neural[session][trial]` equals the raw NWB `Deconvolved` samples at the same curated ROI indices and frame range with `np.allclose`.\n   239→- [ ] Check 2: For chosen trials, verify reconstructed trial start/end frames match raw `trial_start` / `teleport` impulse locations and that converted time starts at 0 s.\n   240→- [ ] Check 3: For chosen trials, verify raw `position` reproduces `absolute_position_bin` and `distance_to_reward_zone_bin` exactly under the planned binning rule.\n   241→- [ ] Check 4: Verify per-trial reward outcome from converted data matches presence/absence of raw `Reward` events within the raw frame/timestamp interval.\n   242→- [ ] Check 5: Verify scene parsing and 30-trial switch logic reproduce per-trial reward-zone labels consistent with paper/code examples.\n   243→\n  379→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   380→\n   381→### Checks Performed\n   382→1. **Output log verification**: `verification_full_out.txt` reports no errors and no warnings.\n   383→2. **Sanity checks NWB export directly. This is a packaging difference, not a semantic one.\n   391→   - `(b) neuron/trial filtering`: converter applies `iscell` and lick-trial QC; reference also mentions a speed-correlation interneuron exclusion. |\n| `get_timeseries_data` | `code/src/reward_relative/glmUtils.py` | PROCESSING | Extracts continuously sampled neural `events` and aligned behavior variables (`rel_pos`, `pos`, `trials`, `speed`, `rewards`) after masking invalid / low-speed / bad-lick samples. |\n| `CircularRegression` / `train_vs_test_blocks` | `code/src/reward_relative/decode.py` | PROCESSING | Decoder used in the paper for circular reward-relative position from deconvolved activity. |\n\n### Notes\n- Reference data flow from code:\n  1. Raw imaging + VR -> `sess` via `create_sess` / `append_session_data`.\n  2. `sess.align_VR_to_2P()` aligns Unity VR to imaging frame times.\n  3. Behavior timeseries added directly from aligned VR (`licks`, `rewards`, `speed`).\n  4. dF/F is not stored in the raw `sess`; it is computed later by `multi_anim_sess` using `preprocessing.dff`.\n  5. Deconvolved calcium `events` are derived from dF/F and used for the decoder notebook.\n- The code is for 2-photon calcium imaging, not electrophysiology.\n- Delta F over F does need to be computed in the original analysis pipeline.\n- Cell curation is not an ephys spike-quality filter. Instead, ROIs are manually curated in the Suite2p GUI, which updates `iscell.npy`; `sess.load_suite2p_data()` loads that curation.\n- `multi_anim_sess_README.md` states each sample is one imaging frame at about 15.5 Hz (~64.5 ms/sample), and `trial_matrices` are spatial bins of 10 cm across 0-450 cm.\n- Decoder-relevant continuous variables in reference code:\n  - neural: `sess.timeseries['events']`\n  - position: `sess.vr_data['pos']`\n  - reward-relative position: computed from reward-zone start position per trial\n  - trial id: repeated sample-wise from trial index\n  - speed: `sess.timeseries['speed']`\n  - rewards: `sess.timeseries['rewards']`\n  - licks: `sess.timeseries['licks']`, with values >1 clipped to 1 and invalid sensors masked\n- Important masking/alignment details from `get_timeseries_data`:\n  - Only samples from `trial_start_inds` to `teleport_inds` are kept.\n  - Trial slices use `start-1:stop-1`, so reference indexing is effectively 1-based for stored trial boundary indices.\n  - Samples with NaNs in `events`, low speed (`<2 cm/s` by default), or invalid lick samples are removed for the paper’s GLM/decoder analyses.\n  - Reward and omission signals are represented as state variables that switch after reward delivery or reward-zone entry, respectively, within a trial.\n- The paper’s Figure 3 decoder notebook decodes circular reward-relative position from deconvolved events, and down-samples observations to match position occupancy across trial sets before training.\n\n---\n\n## Step 2: Dataset `acquisition/TwoPhotonSeries`\n- Representative session structure (`sub-m3_ses-01`):\n  - Behavior time series: `Reward`, `autoreward`, `environment`, `lick`, `position`, `reward_zone`, `scanning`, values `0..8` observed in sample files\n  - `iscell`: shape `(n_rois, 2)` in sample files, consistent with Suite2p export `[is_cell_flag, probability]`\n- Example session (`sub-m3_ses-01`):\n  - -104.5 ± 20.1 cm to +152.7 ± 22.9 cm” |\n\n\n### Processing Details\n- Task structure:\n  - 450 cm linear VR track with hidden 50 cm reward zone.\n  - Reward zones A/B/C are fixed track spans: A `80-130`, B `200-250`, C `320-370` cm.\n  - On switch days, reward zone changes after 30 trials.\n  - ENV2 introduced on day 8; two mice (`m17`, `m18`) started in ENV2.\n- Temporal alignment and sampling:\n  - Neural and behavioral data are analyzed at the imaging frame rate (~15.5 Hz, ~64.5 ms).\n  - Reference code aligns Unity VR data to imaging frames before any later analyses.\n  - For many analyses, only in-trial samples are used; teleport period treatment depends on session/imaging settings.\n- Neural processing:\n  - Fluorescence is motion-corrected and segmented with Suite2P.\n  - dF/F is computed independently within each trial using a maximin baseline with a 20 s window.\n  - dF/F is smoothed with a 2-sample Gaussian.\n  - Deconvolved `events` are extracted with OASIS and are used for decoder and place-cell analyses.\n- Behavioral processing:\n  - Activity at running speeds `<2 cm/s` is excluded from spatial neural analyses and RR decoding.\n  - Licks are binary after correction; corrupted lick trials are set to NaN and excluded from licking analyses.\n  - Reward outcome is trial-specific with random omissions on ~15% of trials.\n- Decoder-specific details:\n  - Target in the paper is circular reward-relative position (`-pi` to `pi`), aligned to reward-zone start.\n  - Tenfold CV, 90% train / 10% test.\n  - Occupancy downsampling matches each RR position bin (`2*pi/45`, about 10 cm).\n  - Shuffles independently circularly shift each neuron’s session timeseries.\n\n### Curation Steps\n\n**Neuron curation rules**:\n- Manual Suite2P curation removes ROIs that:\n  - contain multiple somata or dendrites\n  - lack visually obvious transients\n  - appear overexpressed\n  - show high continuous fluorescence fluctuation typical of putative interneurons\n- Additional putative interneurons are excluded if corr(dF/F, running speed) > 0.5.\n- Multi-plane mice pool planes for most analyses.\n\n**Trial curation rules**:\n- Random reward omissions are part of the task and are not discarded by default.\n- Lick-analysis trials are removed when >30% of imaging-frame samples in the trial have cumulative lick count >2.\n- Many analyses exclude samples with running speed <2 cm/s rather than dropping entire trials.\n- Some analyses use:\n  - pre-switch 30 trials\n  - final 30 post-switch trials\n  - first vs second half of trials on non-switch days\n\n### Decoders Trained\n| Decoded variable | Accuracy |\n| Reward-relative position (paper decoder) | No single scalar value stated in text; qualitatively above shuffle for RR cells after switch, with z-scored decode >2 spanning about -104.5 to +152.7 cm around reward |\n\n---\n\n## Step 4: Check for Consistency\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Discrepancies Found\n| Topic | Code says | Data shows | later sanity-check against raw NWB. |\n| `processing/behavior/BehavioralTimeSeries/position` timestamps | `input[0]` = `time_from_trial_start_s` | `timestamps - timestamps[trial_start]` within each trial | Reference alignment is imaging-frame synchronized behavior | Continuous, time-varying, aligned to trial start as required by decoder task. |\n| `processing/behavior/BehavioralTimeSeries/environment` | `input[1]` = `environment` | Per-trial constant repeated across frames; valid values only (`0/1`) | `behavior.get_trial_types` (`morph`) | Cross-check against scene identifier; encode `ENV1=0`, `ENV2=1`. |\n| Reconstructed within-session trial index | `input[2]` = `trial_number` | 0-based per-trial index repeated across frames | `glmUtils.get_timeseries_data` (`trials`) | Use reconstructed trial order from `trial_start` events, not raw `trial number` during teleport. |\n| Previous trial reward outcome | `input[3]` = `previous_trial_outcome` | For trial `t`, use reward outcome of trial `t-1`; first trial defaults to `0`; repeat across frames | Consistent with task spec; related to `behavior.get_trial_types` | Binary, `0=omitted/unrewarded`, `1=rewarded`. |\n| `position` + trial reward-zone coordinates | `output[0]` = `distance_to_reward_zone_bin` | Signed distance to nearest point in active reward zone: negative before zone, zero inside, positive after zone; discretize into 7 bins from task spec | Paper reward-zone definitions + scene parsing | Use per-trial active zone A/B/C inferred from scene / switch status. |\n| `position` | `output[1]` = `absolute_position_bin` | Bin position on `0-450 cm` track into 5 equal bins | Paper track length 450 cm | Use only in-trial frames; exclude teleport. |\n| `speed` | `output[2]` = `speed_bin` | Discretize cm/s into `<2`, `2-10`, `10-20`, `20-40`, `>40` | `glmUtils.get_timeseries_data` speed stream; paper threshold at 2 cm/s | Do not drop low-speed frames because they are a decoder target class here. |\n| `lick` | `output[3]` = `lick` | Clip cumulative lick counts to binary `0/1`; bad-lick trials dropped by QC rule | `glmUtils.get_timeseries_data`, paper lick QC | Target is time-varying binary lick. |\n| Scene-derived active reward zone | `output[4]` = `reward_zone_location` | Map `A/B/C -> 0/1/2`; repeat across frames in the trial | `behavior.get_reward_zones` | Derived from NWB `identifier` scene string and switch-after-30 rule. |\n| Trial reward delivery from `BehavioralTimeSeries/Reward` | `output[5]` = `reward_outcome` | `1` if any reward event timestamp falls within trial, else `0`; repeat across frames | `behavior.get_trial_types`, `rewardAnalysis.get_reward_inds` | Uses actual delivered reward, matching decoder task. |\n\n### Key Decisions\n1. **Neural signal = deconvolved activity**: The reference decoder and place-cell pipeline use deconvolved calcium activity (`events`). NWB already exports `Deconvolved`, so this is the closest native match.\n2. **Trial-aligned frames exclude teleport period**: Reference code keeps samples from trial start until teleport onset. I will reconstruct trials from `trial_start` impulses to the next `teleport` impulse, excluding teleport frames.\n3. **Use native imaging-frame resolution**: The reference data are sampled at ~15.5 Hz and behavior is already synchronized to this grid. No rebinning in time unless a later validation forces it.\n4. **Per-trial constants will be repeated across time**: Although some decoder variables are per-trial, repeating them across each trial’s time axis gives a uniform `(n_variables, n_timepoints)` representation and matches the decoder’s time-varying format.\n5. **Scene identifier drives reward-zone identity**: NWB `reward_zone` is not the A/B/C label. Active reward-zone identity will be parsed from the session identifier (for example `Env1_LocationA_to_B`, `Env1_B_to_Env2_C`) and the 30-trial switch rule from the paper/code.\n6. **Bad lick trials will be removed**: Because lick is a required decoder output and NaNs are undesirable, trials meeting the paper’s lick-sensor failure rule will be excluded rather than carried with missing labels.\n7. **Initial neuron filter = `iscell[:,0] == 1`**: This matches Suite2p manual curation. Additional interneuron-style exclusions remain a consistency check because of the modest count mismatch with the paper.\n8. **No speed-based sample exclusion in final conversion**: The paper excludes `<2 cm/s` for spatial analyses, but here speed itself is an output class. I will preserve these frames and encode low speed as class `0`.\n9. **Absolute position uses track-only interval**: Position bins will cover the 450 cm corridor only; teleport frames are excluded, so no teleport-specific bins are needed.\n\n### Planned Sanity Checks\n- [ ] Check 1: For a chosen session/trial, verify `neural[session][trial]` equals the raw NWB `Deconvolved` samples at the same curated ROI indices and frame range with `np.allclose`.\n- [ ] Check 2: For chosen trials, verify reconstructed trial start/end frames match raw `trial_start` / `teleport` impulse locations and that converted time starts at 0 s.\n- [ ] Check 3: For chosen trials, verify raw `position` reproduces `absolute_position_bin` and `distance_to_reward_zone_bin` exactly under the planned binning rule.\n- [ 30-trial switch logic reproduce per-trial reward-zone labels consistent with paper/code examples.\n\n---\n\n## Step 6: Script Development\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n- Implemented `convert_data.py` with They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   130→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics such as:\n   131→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   132→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   133→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   134→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   135→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   136→\n   137→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   138→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   139→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   140→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   141→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   142→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   143→\n   144→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   145→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   146→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   147→\n   148→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   149→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   150→\n   151→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   152→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   153→\n   154→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   155→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   156→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   157→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   158→\n   159→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   160→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   161→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   162→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   163→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   164→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   165→\n   166→## Python environment\n   167→\n   168→- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n   169→- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n   170→- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n   171→\n   172→## Decoder Reference\n   173→\n   174→- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   175→- Optional arguments:\n   176→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   177→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   178→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   179→- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n   180→\n   181→### Purpose\n   182→\n   183→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   184→1. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n   189→\n   190→### Consistency requirements\n   191→\n   192→Your processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n   193→- Loading of data\n   194→- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n   195→- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n   196→- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n   197→\n   198→To check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n   199→\n   200→Whenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n   201→\n   202→Discrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n   203→\n   204→Carefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. Be critical of results **after every step of processing**. \n   205→\n   206→You will be assessed on whether the decisions you make on how to load, filter, process, align, reformat, and save the data are reasonable and match the reference paper and code and these instructions. During testing, the reference version of `train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code. \n   207→\n   208→---\n   209→\n   210→## Conversion Workflow\n   211→\n   212→**IMPORTANT**: Follow these steps in order. Each step has explicit completion criteria. Do not proceed to the next step until the current step is complete. Use the exact step names below when documenting progress in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   213→\n   214→**STATUS TRACKING**: At the start of each step, update its status to `IN PROGRESS` in CONVERSION_NOTES.md. When complete, update to `COMPLETE`. This helps you and the user track progress.\n   215→\n   216→---\n   217→\n   218→### Step 0: Setup and Initialization\n   219→\n   220→**Goal**: Set up the working environment and create the notes file.\n   221→\n   222→**Actions**:\n   223→1. **FIRST**: Create `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` with the template structure shown at the end of this document\n   224→2. Verify you can run `python3` and import key packages (numpy, torch)\n   225→3. List the contents of this directory to understand what files are available\n   226→4. **REMINDER**: Do NOT look at any files outside this directory\n   227→\n   228→**Done when**: \n   229→- `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists with the proper template structure\n   230→- You have confirmed Python and key packages work\n   231→- You have listed directory contents in CONVERSION_NOTES.md\n   232→\n   233→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 1, verify that `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists by running `ls -la CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. If it doesn't exist, STOP and create it now.\n   234→\n   235→---\n   236→\n   237→### Step 1: Reference Code Exploration\n   238→\n   239→**⚠️ CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at code in the `code` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\n   240→\n   241→**Goal**: Understand how the original authors loaded and processed their data.\n   242→\n   243→**Actions**:\n   244→- Look for documentation or README files in the `code` directory\n   245→- Read the provided reference code bases to see how the reference paper loaded and manipulated the data\n   246→- Understand which functions are being called to read, curate, process, and manipulate the data\n   247→- For neural imaging data, does delta F over F need to be computed? \n   248→- For electrophysiology neural data, do cells need to be filtered based on quality? \n   249→- Note to file important functions, their inputs, and outputs\n   250→\n   251→**Done when**: You have documented key functions and their purposes in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 1\".\n   252→\n   253→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 2, update Step 1 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   254→\n   255→---\n   256→\n   257→### Step 2: Dataset Exploration\n   258→\n   259→**⚠️ CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at data in the `data` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\n   260→\n   261→**Goal**: Understand the native data structure\n   262→\n   263→**Actions**:\n   264→- Identify how data is organized (all files, formats, hierarchies)\n   265→- Determine available variables and their meanings\n   266→- Check data dimensions and data types\n   267→- Look for documentation or README files\n   268→- Note the data organization and structure\n   269→- Note dataset size parameters such as the **total number** of subjects (mice), sessions, trials, and neurons\n   270→\n   271→**Done when**: You have documented the data structure, file formats, size, and available variables in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 2\".\n   272→\n   273→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 3, update Step 2 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   274→\n   275→---\n   276→\n   277→### Step 3: Reference Text Reading\n   278→\n   279→**Goal**: Extract key information from the paper and methods.\n   280→\n   281→**Actions**:\n   282→- Scan the reference papers and methods.txt for important details about the data\n   283→- Look for and note to file:\n   284→  - Any information about dataset size (number of neurons, subjects, sessions, trials)\n   285→  - Fraction of data with different values, e.g. fraction of rewarded outcomes\n   286→  - Data processing, curation, and filtering\n   287→  - Variables important to experiments\n   288→  - Temporal alignment\n   289→  - Temporal binning\n   290→- Read the reference paper to find information about expected decoder accuracy.\n   291→\n   292→**Done when**: You have documented expected dataset statistics and processing details in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 3\".\n   293→\n   294→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 4, update Step 3 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   295→\n   296→---\n   297→\n   298→### Step 4: Check for Consistency\n   299→\n   300→**Goal**: Verify your understanding is consistent across all sources. \n   301→\n   302→**Actions**:\n   303→- Compare your understanding of:\n   304→  - Important functions from exploring the reference code\n   305→  - Dataset structure from exploring the dataset\n   306→  - Analysis described in the reference texts\n   307→- Note and investigate any discrepancies\n   308→- In particular, look for discrepancies in:\n   309→  - Experimental and behavioral variables\n   310→  - Data processing\n   311→  - Data curation\n   312→- Iterate until your understanding of the reference code, dataset, and reference texts are consistent\n   313→\n   314→**Done when**: You have resolved all discrepancies and documented your final understanding in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 4\".\n   315→\n   316→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 5, update Step 4 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   317→\n   318→---\n   319→\n   320→### Step 5: Mapping Planning\n   321→\n   322→**Goal**: Create a detailed plan for mapping source data to target format.\n   323→\n   324→**Actions**:\n   325→- Map source data elements to target format components:\n   326→  - Which variables contain neural activity?\n   327→  - Identify ALL available experimental variables\n   328→  - Which variables should be decoder **inputs** vs **outputs**? (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   329→  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   330→  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   331→  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\n   332→  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `code` directory. \n   333→- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\n   334→  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\n   335→  - Temporal alignment of trials\n   336→  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\n   337→  - Brain regions recorded from\n   338→- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\n   339→- Check for variables indicating valid data periods — exclude invalid data\n   340→- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\n   341→- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\n   342→\n   343→**Done when**:\n   344→- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \"Step 5\"\n   345→- You have documented all decisions under \"Step 5\"\n   346→- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \"Step 5\"\n   347→\n   348→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   349→\n   350→---\n   351→\n   352→### Step 6: Script Development\n   353→\n   354→**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `convert_data.py`.\n   355→\n   356→**Actions**:\n   357→- Write all conversion code in the file `convert_data.py`\n   358→- Script should run as **`python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\n   359→- Include options:\n   360→  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\n   361→  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\n   362→  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\n   363→- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\n   364→- Write efficient code:\n   365→  - Vectorize loops\n   366→  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\n   367→  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\n   368→  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\n   369→- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\n   370→  - There are no temporal misalignments\n   371→  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\n   372→- Write clear, well-documented functions\n   373→- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\n   374→- Validate data shapes and types at each step\n   375→- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\n   376→\n   377→**Done when**: `convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\n   378→\n   379→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   380→\n   381→---\n   382→\n   383→### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n   384→\n   385→**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\n   386→\n   387→**Actions**:\n   388→1. Run `python -u convert_data.py sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee conversion_sample_out.txt`\n   389→  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\n   390→  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\n   391→  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \n   392→  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\n   393→2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\n   394→  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \n   395→  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \n   396→  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \n   397→3. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_sample_out.txt`.\n   398→4. Analyze the output file `verification_sample_out.txt`:\n   399→  a. Verify no errors reported.\n   400→  b. Attempt to address any warnings\n   401→  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\n   402→\n   403→**Done when**: \n   404→- `sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   405→- `verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   406→- Processing plots show no anomalies\n   407→- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 7\"\n   408→\n   409→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `sample_data.pkl` and `verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \n   410→\n   411→---\n   412→\n   413→### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\n   414→\n   415→**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\n   416→\n   417→**Actions**:\n   418→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\n   419→2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   420→3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\n   421→\n   422→**Investigate any issues**:\n   423→- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\n   424→\n   425→**Done when**: \n   426→- Decoder training completes without errors\n   427→- Loss decreases over epochs\n   428→- Accuracies are above chance\n   429→- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 8\"\n   430→\n   431→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \n   432→\n   433→---\n   434→\n   435→### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\n   436→\n   437→**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\n   438→\n   439→**Actions**:\n   440→1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\n   441→2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\n   442→3. Run `python -u convert_data.py converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee conversion_full_out.txt`\n   443→4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \n   444→5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \n   445→6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \n   446→7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\n   447→8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\n   448→9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\n   449→\n   450→**Done when**:\n   451→- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   452→- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   453→- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\n   454→- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 9\"\n   455→\n   456→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\n   457→\n   458→---\n   459→\n   460→### Step 10: Critical Review 1\n   461→\n   462→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\n   463→\n   464→**Check 1: Output log verification**\n   465→Read through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   466→\n   467→**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\n   468→- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\n   469→- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\n   470→- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \n   471→- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \n   472→- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \n   473→- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   474→\n   475→**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\n   476→- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \n   477→- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \n   478→- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \n   479→- Fix any mismatches. \n   480→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   481→- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\n   482→\n   483→**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\n   484→- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference paper. \n   485→- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \n   486→- Fix any discovered issues. \n   487→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   488→\n   489→**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\n   490→- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \n   491→- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n   492→\n   493→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   494→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   495→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   496→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   497→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   498→\n   499→Write a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n   500→\n   501→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n   502→\n   503→---\n   504→\n   505→### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n   506→\n   507→**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n   508→\n   509→**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n   510→\n   511→**Actions**:\n   512→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n   513→2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n   514→3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   515→4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the paper. \n   516→\n   517→**Done when**: \n   518→1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n   519→2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n   520→\n   521→---\n   522→\n   523→### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n   524→\n   525→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n   526→\n   527→**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n   528→- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n   529→- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n   530→- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n   531→\n   532→**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to paper**\n   533→- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference paper\n   534→- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the paper's accuracy for each variable\n   535→- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n   536→- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n   537→\n   538→**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n   539→- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n   540→\n   541→**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n   542→1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n   543→2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n   544→3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n   545→4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n   546→5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and paper. \n   547→\n   548→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   549→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   550→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   551→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   552→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   553→\n   554→**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n   555→\n   556→**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n   557→\n   558→---\n   559→\n   560→### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n   561→\n   562→**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n   563→\n   564→**Actions**:\n   565→- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n   566→  - Key decisions and rationale\n   567→  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n   568→  - Validation results and tables\n   569→  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n   570→- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\n   571→  - Dataset description\n   572→  - How to load and use the converted data\n   573→  - Output format specification\n   574→  - Key statistics\n   575→- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\n   576→- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n   577→\n   578→**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n   579→\n   580→---\n   581→\n   582→## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n   583→\n   584→**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. Do this BEFORE exploring any code or data. Document everything in this file as you work.\n\n2. **FOLLOW STEPS IN ORDER**: Do not skip steps. Do not proceed to step N+1 until step N is complete. Each step has \"Done when\" criteria — verify them before moving on.\n\n3. **MATCH THE REFERENCE PROCESSING**: Your processing must match what's described in the reference paper and code. You will be assessed on this consistency.\n\n---\n\n## Project Context\n\n- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference paper and code repository. \n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your correctness on **matching the loading and processing described in the reference texts and code**. \n\n## Reference Information\n\n- The reference **paper** \"A flexible hippocampal population code for experience relative to reward\" is in the file `paper.pdf`.\n- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`. \n- **Code** from the paper are in the directory `code`.\n- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `data`.\n\n## Decoder Task\n\nTemporally align based on start of the trial. \n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time from start of trial in seconds (continuous, time-varying)\n- Environment type (binary, ENV1 vs ENV2, per trial)\n- Trial number (continuous, per trial)\n- Previous trial outcome (binary, omitted = 0, rewarded = 1, per trial)\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Distance to any location in the reward zone, time-varying. Discretization bins:\n  - 0:  < -50 cm\n  - 1: -50 to -10 cm\n  - 2: -10 cm to < 0 cm\n  - 3: 0 cm\n  - 4: >0 cm to +10 cm\n  - 5: +10 to +50 cm\n  - 6: > +50 cm\n- Absolute position in corridor, discretized into 5 equal-sized bins, time-varying\n- Speed, time-varying. Discretized into bins:\n  - 0: < 2 cm/s\n  - 1: 2-10 cm/s\n  - 2: 10-20 cm/s\n  - 3: 20-40 cm/s\n  - 4: > 40 cm/s\n- Lick, time-varying. 0 = no, 1 = yes\n- Reward zone location, per-trial. 0 = A, 1 = B, 2 = C\n- Reward outcome, per-trial. 0 = no, 1 = yes\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ] # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n\nCarefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. Be critical of results **after every step of processing**. \n\nYou will be assessed on whether the decisions you make on how to load, filter, process, align, reformat, and save the data are reasonable and match the reference paper and code and these instructions. During testing, the reference version of `train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\n\n**Check 1: Output log verification**\nRead through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n\n**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\n- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\n- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\n- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \n- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \n- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \n- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n\n**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\n- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \n- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \n- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \n- Fix any mismatches. \n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\n\n**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\n- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference paper. \n- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \n- Fix any discovered issues. \n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n\n**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\n- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \n- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n\nWrite a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n\n**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n\n---\n\n### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n\n**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n\n**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n\n**Actions**:\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the paper. \n\n**Done when**: \n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n\n---\n\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to paper**\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference paper\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the paper's accuracy for each variable\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and paper. \n\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n\n**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n\n---\n\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n\n**Actions**:\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n  - Key decisions and rationale\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n  - Validation results and tables\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\n  - Dataset description\n  - How to load and use the converted data\n  - Output format specification\n  - Key statistics\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\n- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n\n---\n\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. max]}\n   187→        reference_ranges: dict of {name: [min, max]}\n   188→\n   189→    Returns:\n   190→        matches: list of (ref_name, sub_name, cost) tuples\n   191→        cost_matrix: the 31→| `create_sess` | `code/src/reward_relative/preprocessing.py` | LOADING | Creates a `sess` object from scanbox/VR/session metadata and calls alignment + behavior loading. |\n    32→| `code/src/reward_relative/preprocessing.py` | PROCESSING | Computes trial-masked dF/F from fluorescence, with neuropil subtraction and optional deconvolution to `events`. |\n    34→| `multi_anim_sess` | `code/src/reward_relative/utilities.py` | PROCESSING | Loads per-session `sess` pickles, computes dF/F and `events`, reward zones, trial subsets, and place-cell outputs across animals. |\n    35→|
